Theorem 32
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an essential ancestral graph and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V,E^{\prime}})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph, such that
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same skeleton, the same set of minimal collider paths, and all invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}
are identical in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Suppose furthermore, that orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
,
R8
,
R11
. If there are no edges of the form
A\leftrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that correspond to
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}
, then the following hold:

(i)

For any edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
, there is a MAG
\mathcal{M}_{1}
represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
.

(ii)

For any edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, there are three MAGs
\mathcal{M}_{1}
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}
and
\mathcal{M}_{3}
represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
,
A\leftarrow B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
, and
A\leftrightarrow B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{3}
.

(iii)

For any edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that corresponds to
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}
, there are two MAGs
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
, and
A\leftrightarrow B
is in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
.

Consider the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
in Figure
10
(a), as well as the partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in Figure
10
(c) which can be obtained as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\texttt{{addOrientationKnowledge}}(\mathcal{G},\{H\to G\})
. Then
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
satisfy assumptions of Theorem
32
.
To show that Theorem
32
holds, it is enough to show that there is a MAG represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that contains
E\to G
and such that a desired edge from the following set
\langle A,B\rangle,\langle B,C\rangle,\langle C,D\rangle
is oriented as
\to,\leftarrow
, or
\leftrightarrow.

We will rely on Theorem
30
. Namely, it is enough to show that we can orient all the remaining
[formula]
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that also correspond to
[formula]
in
\mathcal{G}
as
\to
without incurring additional orientations or creating an almost directed cycle or a new minimal collider path. This is similar to a proof strategy used by
Zhang [2008b]
for essential ancestral graphs, except that we have additional orientation rules and already incorporated orientation knowledge to consider. We show that indeed holds in Theorem
68
. For
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in Figure
10
(c), it is almost immediately apparent that orienting
E\to G
does not incur any ancestral issues, new minimal collider paths, or edge orientations. Additionally, once
E\to G
is oriented, our new partial mixed graph satisfies Theorem
30
and hence, the rest of the claim follows.

Corollary 33
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an essential ancestral graph and
\mathcal{K}
be a set of orientation knowledge consistent with
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\texttt{{addOrientationKnowledge}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
.
If there are no
A\leftrightarrow B
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that correspond to
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}
, then Theorem
32
holds for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Unfortunately, our orientation rules are not necessarily complete in the general setting. In the absence of proof of general completeness,
we devise the
verifyCompleteness
algorithm for checking whether a partial mixed graph is a restricted essential ancestral graph. The pseudocode of algorithm
verifyCompleteness

is given in Algorithm
3
.
Algorithm
3
relies on the results of Theorem
30
and Lemmas
24
and
28
to verify soundness and completeness of orientations in a partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
obtained from an essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
, orientation knowledge
\mathcal{K}
, through
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\texttt{{addOrientationKnowledge}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
. The algorithm returns TRUE if
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the
\mathcal{K}
-restricted essential ancestral graph and FALSE otherwise.

To explain the reasoning in more detail, let
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
, be the set of all
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
edges which are in both
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
If
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
or if we have reached Line
35
of Algorithm
3
, we only need to check that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is ancestral and has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}
, which is done in Line
36
. If this check is passed, we have that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
satisfies Theorem
30
relative to
\mathcal{G}
, and so it is a restricted essential ancestral graph, and Algorithm
3
returns TRUE. Otherwise, Algorithm
3
returns FALSE.

If
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, we enter Line
8
of Algorithm
3
. Now it suffices to verify that for all
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
edges in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
, there are graphs
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
such that where
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
A\leftrightarrow B
is in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
and such that all invariant edgemarks in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are identical in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
,
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
individually satisfy Theorem
30
relative to
\mathcal{G}
. Note that for
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
to satisfy Theorem
30
relative to
\mathcal{G}
all variant edge marks from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
must be invariant in these graphs. This check is done between the lines
7
and
35
. Note that this means we do not necessarily need to construct graphs for every combination of edge orientations in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
. Rather, if
|\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}|=k
, we need to construct
2k
graphs between the lines
7
and
35
.

Importantly, in Line
24
, we also check that across the creation of these
2k
graphs, we do not always encounter some other invariant edge mark in the former circle component of
\mathcal{G}
that is labeled as a variant in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Having such an invariant edge mark across all
2k
graphs does not immediately indicate an issue, as we did not check exhaustively over all combinations of edge orientations on
[formula]
edges from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
. However,
we need to perform an additional check, making sure that the complements of those orientations are viable, which we do between Lines
24
and
34
. By complementary orientation, we mean that if
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
was always encountered in these
2k
graphs, we check that
C\to D
is viable, and if
C\to D
was encountered across all graphs, we check that
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is a viable orientation.

6.4
Simulation Results

(a)

(b)

Figure 11
:
(a) Average runtime of Algorithm
3
for various
n
and percentage of revealed
\circ
edge marks in
p=0.05
regime.
(b) Average runtime of Algorithm
3
for various
n
and
p
, under a fixed percentage of revealed
\circ
edge marks.

We perform simulations to explore the runtime of Algorithm
3
. Our simulations used
R
v4.3.0 and
pcalg
v2.7-8 on a CPU with 4 cores and 30 GB RAM limit. Our implementation of Algorithms
1
-
3
is available through
R
package,
expertOrientR
, on GitHub (
https://github.com/AparaV/expertOrientR
).

We start by generating DAGs using the
randomDAG
function from the
pcalg
package with the Erdős-Rényi
G(n,p)
model, where
n
is the number of nodes and
p
is the probability of an edge existing between two nodes. We generate 1000 DAGs for each combination of
n\in\{10,12,15,20,25,30,35,40\}
and
p\in\{0.05,0.1,0.25\}
. For each generated DAG
\mathcal{D}
, we randomly select
10\%
of its source nodes to be designated as latent and construct the corresponding MAG
\mathcal{M}
on the observed nodes. The MAGs
\mathcal{M}
generated in this way contain, on average, 1-2 fewer nodes compared to the original DAGs, and the probability of an edge existing between two nodes in
\mathcal{M}
is on average
\{0.055,0.11,0.295\}
for the corresponding DAG settings. We also construct the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{M}
. For each
\mathcal{G}
generated in this fashion, we choose
k\%
of
\circ
edge marks,
k\in\{10,30,50,80\}
, in
\mathcal{G}
to reveal as orientation knowledge
\mathcal{K}
, using the true edge marks in
\mathcal{M}
. If one of the edge marks we choose to reveal is a tail, we also reveal the arrowhead edge mark on the same edge in
\mathcal{K}
. We then obtain the partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\texttt{{addOrientationKnowledge}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K})
. Now, for each combination of
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{K}
, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
we run
verifyCompleteness
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and record its runtime. In all of our simulations,
verifyCompleteness
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
has never returned FALSE.

We report the average runtime in seconds as a function of
n
,
p
, and orientation knowledge percentage in the two plots in Figure
11
. In Figure
11
(a), we can see the average runtime in seconds as a function of
n
and orientation knowledge percentage for a fixed
p
,
p=0.05
. As expected, the algorithm’s runtime increases with graph size
n
, though we can notice that the runtime can be improved by revealing more orientation knowledge.
In Figure
11
(b), we can see the average runtime in seconds as a function of
n
and
p
when about
30\%
of circle edge marks are revealed by orientation knowledge. In this plot, it is clear that the starting DAG density has an enormous impact on algorithm runtime. This is because the size and density of a generated DAG affect the size and density of the associated MAG. In turn, the MAG influences the size of the Markov equivalence class of the essential ancestral graph. For dense and large graphs, this class can be substantial. As a result, verifying completeness becomes computationally challenging (see Figure 4 of
Wang et al., 2024a
for a simulation investigating sizes of the Markov equivalence classes of MAGs).

In Section
E.3
, we perform additional simulations comparing how often the true restricted essential graph agrees with the restricted essential graph learned from finite data.

7
Discussion

We considered using expert knowledge of edge marks from the true MAG to restrict a Markov equivalence class. We refer to this type of expert knowledge as
orientation knowledge
. Orientation knowledge is more general compared to tiered or local knowledge, when imposed on existing edges in the graph, in that it allows specifying bidirected edges, but also does not require all edge marks incident to a node to be specified
[
Andrews et al., 2020
,
Mooij et al., 2020
,
Wang et al., 2022
,
Wang et al., 2023
,
Wang et al., 2024b
]
. Our results bridge several characterizations of Markov equivalence (Section
3
), and we provide several new graphical orientation rules for restricting such a class (Section
5
).
We construct an algorithm to add orientation knowledge into an essential ancestral graph (Algorithm
2
) and show that it is complete in specific settings (Section
6
) by generalizing results of
Meek [1995]
and
Zhang [2008b]
. Outside of these settings, we devise an algorithm (Algorithm
3
) to check whether the output of our Algorithm
2
is complete (Section
6.3
) and discuss its runtime (Section
6.4
).

Proving a general completeness result for a partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is challenging due to the existence of bidirected edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that correspond to
[formula]
edges in the essential ancestral graph. One strategy employed by
Zhang [2008b]
to show the completeness of rules for constructing an essential ancestral graph involves considering the circle and non-circle components separately. We use a similar strategy for our results in Section
6
. However, this approach does not work in general.
For instance, consider the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
in Figure
12
(a) and a partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
constructed as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\texttt{addBgKnowedge}(\mathcal{G},\{B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D\})
in Figure
12
(b). Graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a restricted essential ancestral graph as
verifyCompleteness
(
\mathcal{G},\langle\{B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D\},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
returns TRUE.
It is impossible to orient all remaining
[formula]
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as
\to
without incurring a new unshielded collider
\langle A,D,C\rangle
. Furthermore, orienting either
A\to B
or
C\to B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
leads to orientations on
\langle A,D\rangle
and
\langle D,C\rangle
edges.

Another strategy for showing the completeness of orientation rules employed by
Meek [1995]
and Theorem
29
above relies on exploiting properties of chordal graphs. However,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
will not generally be a chordal graph. See, for instance,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in Figure
6
(a) and, in particular, the cycle
C\leftrightarrow D\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}F\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
which is not chordal.

A
B
D
C

(a)

A
B
D
C

(b)

Figure 12
:
(a) An essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
, (b) a restricted essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

The general completeness problem remains open.
In our simulations, we never encounter a case where our orientation rules are incomplete; that is, we never observe a case where
verifyCompleteness
outputs a FALSE given an essential ancestral graph and consistent orientation knowledge.
However,
Wang et al. [2025]
discovered R14, applicable when assumptions of Theorem
32
are violated (when bidirected edges exist in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that correspond to
[formula]
edges in
\mathcal{G}
), while our work was under review. This suggests that cases of incompleteness occur in graphs that are difficult to elicit through simulations. This is bolstered by the fact that the simplest examples of these new rules (our
R4
and
R13
, and
Wang et al. [2025]
’s R14) require dense graphical models that are challenging to generate.

We note that our paper does not cover the topics of causal effect identification or estimation given a restricted essential ancestral graph. Instead, we leave these questions open for future investigations.
We also believe that some of our results should help improve causal discovery and potentially, for proving consistency results for existing causal discovery algorithms
[
Triantafillou and Tsamardinos, 2016
,
Rantanen et al., 2021
,
Claassen and Bucur, 2022
,
Hu and Evans, 2024
]
. The remaining open questions also include considerations of expert knowledge in the presence of selection bias.

Appendix A
Additional Preliminaries and Existing Results

We denote sets of nodes in bold (for example
\mathbf{V}
), graphs in calligraphic font (for example
\mathcal{G}
) and nodes in a graph in uppercase letters (for example
V
).

Paths.
A
path

p
from
A
to
B
in
\mathcal{G}
is a sequence of distinct nodes
\langle A,\dots,B\rangle
on which every pair of successive nodes are adjacent in
\mathcal{G}
.
If
p=\langle V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k},\rangle,k\geq 2
, then
V_{1}
and
V_{k}
are
endpoints
of
p
, and any other node
V_{i},1<i<k,
is a
non-endpoint
node on
p
.
The
length
of a path
p
, labeled
|p|
equals the number of edges on
p
.
A
subsequence
of path
p
is a sequence of nodes obtained by deleting some nodes from
p
without changing the order of the remaining nodes.
For a path
p=\langle V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{m}\rangle
, the
subpath
from
V_{i}
to
V_{k}
(
1\leq i\leq k\leq m)
is the path
p(V_{i},V_{k})=\langle V_{i},V_{i+1},\dots,V_{k}\rangle
.
If
p=\langle V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k},\rangle,k\geq 2
, then with
-p
we denote the path
\langle V_{k},\dots,V_{2},V_{1}\rangle
.
For two disjoint subsets
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
of
\mathbf{V}
, a path from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{B}
is a path from some
A\in\mathbf{A}
to some
B\in\mathbf{B}
.
If
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{*}
are two graphs with identical adjacencies and
p
is a path in
\mathcal{G}
, then the
corresponding path

{p}^{*}
is the path in
\mathcal{G}^{*}
constituted by the same sequence of nodes as
p
.

Concatenation of paths.
We denote the concatenation of paths by
\oplus
, so that for example
p=p(V_{1},V_{k})\oplus p(V_{k},V_{m})
. In this paper, we only concatenate paths if the result of the concatenation is again a path.

Definition 34

(Chordal Graph)
.

Graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
is chordal if for every path
p=\langle V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k}\rangle
,
k>3
in
\mathcal{G}
such that edge
\langle V_{1},V_{k}\rangle
is also in
\mathcal{G}
, there is an edge
\langle V_{i},V_{j}\rangle
,
1\leq i<j\leq k
in
\mathcal{G}
, such that
j-i>1
.

A.1
Existing Results

Theorem 35

(Theorem 2.1 of
Zhao et al., 2005
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
be two MAGs on the same set of nodes
\mathbf{V}
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are Markov equivalent if and only if

\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
have the same skeleton and the same minimal collider paths.

Lemma 36

(c.f. Lemmas 4.1, A.1, B.7, and B.8 of
Zhang, 2008b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph. Then, the circle component of
\mathcal{G}
i.e., a subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
containing only edges of type
is a union of disconnected chordal graphs
\mathcal{G}_{C_{1}},\dots,\mathcal{G}_{C_{k}}
,
k\geq 1
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
is an induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 37

(Lemmas B.4, B.5 and Corollary B.6 of
Zhang, 2008b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph. If path
p=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\rangle,k>1
, does not contain any edge of the form
V_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{i+1},1\leq i\leq k-1
and if there is an edge
\langle V_{1},V_{k}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}
, then
V_{1}\to V_{k}
, or
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
Furthermore, if
V_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
V_{1}\to V_{k}
, or
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 38

(Lemmas B.7 of
Zhang, 2008b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph. If path
p=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\rangle,k>1
, is of the form
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}\dots\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{k}
and there is an edge
\langle V_{1},V_{k}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}
, then
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}.

Lemma 39

(Lemmas B.8 of
Zhang, 2008b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph. If path
p=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\rangle,k>3
, is an unshielded path of the form
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}\dots\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{k}
in
\mathcal{G}
, then there is no edge
\langle V_{i},V_{j}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}
, where
1\leq i<j\leq k.

Lemma 40

(Lemma A.1 of
Zhang, 2008b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph, and let
A,B,
and
C
be three distinct nodes in
\mathcal{G}.
If
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is also in
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, if
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{G},
then
A\to C
, or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}.

Lemma 41

(Lemma 7.5 of
Maathuis and Colombo, 2015
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be two distinct nodes in an essential graph
\mathcal{G}
. If edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
then any path
p=\langle A=V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k}=B\rangle,k>1
from
A
to
B
, must contain at least one edge of the form
V_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{i+1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
.
Conversely, if a path
q=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{r}\rangle,r>1
does not contain any edge of the form
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,r-1\},
then
q
is a possibly directed path from
V_{1}
to
V_{r}
.

Appendix B
Auxiliary Results

We first generalize a few important and well known essential ancestral graph properties to our general partial mixed graph setting. Corollary
42
generalizes Lemma B.1 of
Zhang [2008b]
, Corollary
43
generalizes Lemma B.2 of
Zhang [2008b]
, and Lemma
44
generalizes Lemma 1 of
Meek [1995]
and Lemma A.1 of
Zhang [2008b]
(given in Lemma
40
above).

Corollary 42
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partial mixed graph.
Let
p=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{k}\rangle,k>1
be a possibly directed path in
\mathcal{G}
. Then there is a subsequence of
p
called
p^{\prime}
,
p^{\prime}=\langle V_{1}=V^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{2},\dots,V^{\prime}_{\ell}=V_{k}\rangle
,
\ell>1
, such that
p^{\prime}
is an unshielded possibly directed path.

Observe that any subsequence of
p
that is a path would necessarily be a possibly directed path. Hence, if
p
is not unshielded, we can obtain
p^{\prime}
through an iterative process of skipping over shielded nodes.

Corollary 43
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partial mixed graph such that the orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under rule
R1
.
Let
p=\langle A,\dots,B\rangle
be an unshielded possibly directed path in
\mathcal{G}
. Then:

(i)

If there is a
[formula]
or
\to
edge on
p
, then all edges after that edge on
p
are of type
\to

(ii)

If there is a
edge on
p
, this edge occurs before a
[formula]
or
\to
edge on
p
.

(iii)

There is at most one
[formula]
edge on
p

Follows from the fact that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
and the fact that
p
is an unshielded possibly directed path.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partial mixed graph such that the orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under rules
R1
and
R2
. For any three nodes
A,B,C\in\mathcal{G}
such that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
. Then there is an edge between
A
and
C
in
\mathcal{G}
that is not of the form
A\leftarrow C
. Moreover, if
A\to B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then the edge between
A
and
C
is also not of the form
A\leftrightarrow C
.

Since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
, there must be an edge between
A
and
C
.
The edge between
A
and
C
cannot be of the form
A\leftarrow C
, since that would imply that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are not closed under
R2
. Similarly, if the edge between
A
and
B
in
\mathcal{G}
is
A\to B
, then due to
R2
,
A\leftrightarrow C
is also not in
\mathcal{G}
.

Appendix C
Supplement to Section
3

Let
C=Q_{k}
. To begin, we note that
p
forms a collider path in
\mathcal{M}
. If
p
is a minimal collider path, then
\langle Q_{k-1},C,B\rangle
will be a collider in every
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that is Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
(Theorem
35
).

Otherwise,
p
is not a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{M}
. Then there is a subsequence
p^{\prime}=\langle A=Q_{n_{0}},Q_{n_{1}},\dots,Q_{n_{m}},C,B\rangle
of
p
in
\mathcal{M}
, such that
p^{\prime}
is a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{M}
and if
m>0
,
\{Q_{n_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{m}\subset\{Q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
. Note that
B
must be in
p^{\prime}
as
Q_{i}\to B
for all
i
by definition of discriminating path. There are two possibilities for
Q_{n_{m}}
on
p^{\prime}

(i)

Q_{n_{m}}=Q_{k-1}
: Then, by Theorem
35
,
\langle Q_{k-1},C,B\rangle
forms a collider in every MAG
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that is Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
.

(ii)

n_{m}<k-1
: Then, we have
Q_{n_{m}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{M}
. By Theorem
35
, we have
Q_{n_{m}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in every
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that is Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
. So, we now only need to show that
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in every
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that is Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that there is at least one MAG Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
that does not contain
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
. Therefore, in the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{M}
, we have
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
. Then by Lemma
40
, the edge between
Q_{k-1}
and
B
of the form
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{k-1}
. This is a contradiction to the assumption that
p
is a discriminating path in
\mathcal{M}
as that implies that
Q_{k-1}
is a parent of
B
in
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, we must have
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
in the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
and therefore,
\langle Q_{k-1},C,B\rangle
is a collider in every
\mathcal{M}^{*}
that is Markov equivalent to
\mathcal{M}
.

We will use the following notation: if
p_{\mathcal{M}}
is a path in
\mathcal{M}
, then
p_{\mathcal{G}}
denotes the corresponding path in
\mathcal{G}
.
Based on the construction of
\mathcal{G}
by Algorithm
1
, we know that all minimal collider paths in
\mathcal{M}
are also in
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, any edge orientation done by Algorithm
1
should match the same edge orientation in
\mathcal{M}
as long as the use of
Zhao-R4
does not induce a different orientation in
\mathcal{G}
.
Hence, for
\mathcal{G}
to be an essential ancestral graph of
\mathcal{M}
, it is sufficient to show that all colliders discriminated by a path
p_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\mathcal{M}
are also colliders on
p_{\mathcal{G}}
in
\mathcal{G}
(see also Theorem
2
).

Hence, consider a discriminating path
p_{\mathcal{M}}=\langle A=Q_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},B\rangle
,
k\geq 2
in
\mathcal{M}
such that
Q_{k}
is a collider on
p_{\mathcal{M}}.

If
p_{\mathcal{M}}
is a minimal collider path, then
Q_{k}
is a collider on
p_{\mathcal{G}}
and we are done. Hence, for the rest of the proof suppose that
p_{\mathcal{M}}
is not a minimal collider path, and let
p_{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
be a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that forms a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{M}
.

Since
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{M})
and since
Q_{i}\to B
is in
\mathcal{M}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
it follows
\langle Q_{k},B\rangle
is on
p_{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
, that is,
p_{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
is of the form
p_{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}=\langle A=Q_{n_{0}},Q_{n_{1}},\dots,Q_{n_{\ell}},Q_{k},B\rangle
,
\ell\geq 0
. Let
p_{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
be the corresponding minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
Q_{k}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and we only need to show that
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{k}
is also in
\mathcal{G}
. Of course, this immediately holds if
Q_{n_{\ell}}=Q_{k-1}
, so for the rest of the proof consider the case where
Q_{k-1}
is not on
p_{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
.

Note that since,
Q_{k}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
for an arbitrarily chosen discriminating collider path
\langle A=Q_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},B\rangle
in
\mathcal{M}
, we can conclude that all orientations made by completing the
Zhao-R4
in Algorithm
1
match the orientations on the corresponding edge in
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, since we know that
Q_{k-1}\leftrightarrow Q_{k}
is in
\mathcal{M}
, we know that
Q_{k-1}\leftarrow Q_{k}
, or
Q_{k-1}\to Q_{k}
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}
.
Furthermore, this implies that
\mathcal{G}
is an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain any inducing paths.

Now, to show that
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{k}
or
Q_{k-1}\leftrightarrow Q_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, we show that the other remaining option for this edge in
\mathcal{G}
:
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{k}
leads to a contradiction. Hence, suppose that
Q_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{k}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then since
p_{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}=\langle A=Q_{n_{0}},Q_{n_{1}},\dots,Q_{n_{\ell}},Q_{k},B\rangle
is a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
, such that
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G})
and
Q_{k-1}
is not on
p_{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
, but
Q_{k}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{k-1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
45
would imply that
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{k-1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. However, this now leads us to a contradiction with the fact that invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}
match those in
\mathcal{M}
, since we know that
B\leftarrow Q_{k-1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
.

Note that
p
is of the form
P_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}P_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow P_{k-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}P_{k}
and that
P_{1}\notin\Adj(P_{k},\mathcal{G})
by definition. Hence, if
k=3
, the claim holds by definition.

For the rest of the proof suppose that
k>3
and let
i\in\{2,\dots,k-1\}
. If
P_{i-1}\notin\Adj(P_{i+1},\mathcal{G})
, then we are in case
(i)
and we are done.
Otherwise,
P_{i-1}\in\Adj(P_{i+1},\mathcal{G})
, so by
(iii)
of Lemma
47
, we have that either
P_{i-1}\to P_{i+1}
or
P_{i-1}\leftarrow P_{i+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}.

Assume without loss of generality that
P_{i-1}\to P_{i+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. We will show that in this case, we end up having a discriminating collider path for
P_{i}
of the form in
(ii)
. If
P_{i-1}\leftarrow P_{i+1}
was in
\mathcal{G}
, an analogous argument can be used to show the existence of a discriminating collider path for
P_{i}
of the form in
(iii)
.

Since
P_{i-1}\to P_{i+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, by
(i)
of Lemma
47
, we have that
i-1\neq 1
, that is
i>2
. If
i=3
, we must have that
P_{i-2}\notin\Adj(P_{i+1},\mathcal{G})
, by
(i)
and
(iv)
of Lemma
47
, and in this case we immediately have that
p(P_{i-2},P_{i+1})
is a discriminating collider path of the form
(ii)
.

Otherwise,
i>3
, and either
P_{i-2}\notin\Adj(P_{i+1},\mathcal{G})
, in which case we again have that
p(P_{i-2},P_{i+1})
is a discriminating collider path of the form
(ii)
, or
P_{i-2}\to P_{i+1}
by
(iv)
of Lemma
47
. Now, we can apply the above argument iteratively, since if
i=4
, we have that
P_{i-3}\notin\Adj(P_{i+1},\mathcal{G})
by
(i)
and
(iv)
of Lemma
47
, and otherwise, we have that
P_{i-3}\to P_{i+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
and we consider the presence of edge
\langle P_{i-4},P_{i+1}\rangle
.

.

Lemma 45
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths and such that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
-
R3
,
Zhao-R4
. Furthermore, let
A
and
B
be distinct nodes in
\mathcal{G}
such that
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G})
. Suppose that there is a minimal collider path
p=\langle A=Q_{l_{k}},\dots,Q_{l_{1}},Q,Q_{r_{1}},\dots,Q_{r_{m}}=B\rangle
,
k,m\geq 1
, in
\mathcal{G}
and a node
W
not on
p
such that
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then the following hold:

(i)

Either
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, or
k>1
and there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
such that
Q_{l_{i}}\leftrightarrow W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

(ii)

Either
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, or
m>1
and there is an
j\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
such that
Q_{r_{j}}\leftrightarrow W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

First note, that by Lemma
51
, we have that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}

.

Definition 46

(Distance to
\mathbf{Z}
; cf.
Zhang, 2006
,
Perković et al., 2018
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partial mixed graph,
p
a path in
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathbf{Z}\subset\mathbf{V}
. Suppose that every node on
p=\langle V_{1},\dots V_{k}\rangle
is in
\PossAn(\mathbf{Z},\mathcal{G})
. Then the distance to
\mathbf{Z}
for each node
V_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
on
p
is the length of a shortest possibly causal path from
V_{i}
to
\mathbf{Z}
. The distance to
\mathbf{Z}
for the entire path
p
is equal to the sum of the distances to
\mathbf{Z}
for each node on
p
.

The following Lemma is similar to Lemma 2.1 of
Zhao et al. [2005]
.

Lemma 47
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph and let
p
, be a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
,
p=\langle A=Q_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},Q_{k+1}=B\rangle,k\geq 2
. Furthermore, suppose that the edge orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
Zhao-R4
.
Then the following hold

(i)

If edge
\langle Q_{i},A\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}
for some
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
, then this edge is of the form
Q_{i}\to A
.

(ii)

If edge
\langle Q_{i},B\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, then it is of the form
Q_{i}\to B
.

(iii)

If edge
\langle Q_{i},Q_{j}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}
for some
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\},i<j-1
, then this edge is either
Q_{i}\to Q_{j}
or
Q_{j}\to Q_{i}
.

(iv)

If
\langle Q_{i},Q_{j}\rangle
and
\langle Q_{i},Q_{j+1}\rangle
are edges in
\mathcal{G}
for some
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\},i\neq j
, then these edges are either
Q_{j}\to Q_{i}\leftarrow Q_{j+1}
, or
Q_{j}\leftarrow Q_{i}\to Q_{j+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Note that since
p
is a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
, we have that
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G}).

(i)
,
(ii)

We only prove the claim
(i)
, since the proof for claim
(ii)
is symmetric. Note that the edge
\langle Q_{i},A\rangle
cannot be of the form
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}A
, since in this case,
p
is not a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, we only need to show that this edge is also not of the form
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}A
.

Since
Q_{k+1}=B
is not adjacent to
A
in
\mathcal{G}
, there is at least one node on
p(Q_{i+1},Q_{k+1})
that is not adjacent to
A
. Let
Q_{r},i<r\leq k+1
be the closest node to
Q_{i}
on
p(Q_{i},Q_{k+1})
such that
Q_{r}\notin\Adj(A,\mathcal{G})
.
Then
Q_{j}\in\Adj(A,\mathcal{G})
for all
j\in\{i,\dots,r-1\}
. Additionally,
Q_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}A
is not in
\mathcal{G}
for any
j\in\{i,\dots,r-1\}
as that would contradict that
p
is a minimal collider path. If
Q_{r-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}A
was in
\mathcal{G}
,
Q_{r}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{r-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}A
and

would contradict Lemma
44
. Hence,
A\leftarrow Q_{r-1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

If
i=r-1
we are done.
Otherwise, consider the path
Q_{r}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{r-1}\leftrightarrow Q_{r-2}
and edge
Q_{r-1}\to A
in
\mathcal{G}
. Since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
Zhao-R4
and since

is in
\mathcal{G}
. We can apply this same reasoning iteratively for all (if any) remaining

to show that
Q_{j}\to A
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

(iii)

Since
p
is a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
, it is clear that
Q_{i}\leftrightarrow Q_{j}
is not in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, we only need to show that
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{j}
and
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{j}
are not in
\mathcal{G}
. We will do this by contradiction.

Suppose first that
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{j}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Since
i\geq 1
,
Q_{i-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{i}

,
Q_{i-1}\to Q_{j}
,

, and or

Lemma 48
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths. Furthermore, suppose that the edge orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under rules
R1
,
R2
, and
Zhao-R4
, and let
p=\langle A=Q_{0},Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},Q_{k+1}=B\rangle,k\geq 2
be a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
.
Then the following hold

(i)

For any subpath
p(Q_{i},Q_{j}),0\leq i<j-1\leq k
, there is at least one non-endpoint node
Q_{l}
,
l\in\{i+1,\dots,j-1\}
such that
Q_{l}\notin\An(\{Q_{i},Q_{j}\},\mathcal{G})
.

(ii)

There is at least one unshielded triple on
p
.

(iii)

Suppose that there is an edge
Q_{i}\to Q_{j}
,
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k+1\}
,
i<j
in
\mathcal{G}
. Then there is a node
Q_{l}
,
0\leq l<i
, such that
Q_{l}\notin\Adj(Q_{j},\mathcal{G})
and
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{j}
is in
\mathcal{G}
for all
l_{1}\in\{l+1,\dots,i\}
.

(iv)

Suppose that there is an edge
Q_{i}\leftarrow Q_{j}
,
i,j\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
,
i<j
in
\mathcal{G}
. Then there is a node
Q_{r}
,
j<r\leq k+1
, such that
Q_{r}\notin\Adj(Q_{i},\mathcal{G})
and
Q_{r_{1}}\to Q_{i}
is in
\mathcal{G}
for all
r_{1}\in\{j,\dots,r-1\}
.

Since
p
is a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{G}
,
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G})
.

(i)

Suppose for a contradiction that there is a subpath
p(Q_{i},Q_{j})
, of
p
such that for all
l\in\{i+1,\dots,j-1\}
,
Q_{l}\in\An(\{Q_{i},Q_{j}\},\mathcal{G})
. Since there are no inducing paths in
\mathcal{G}
,
Q_{i}\in\Adj(Q_{j},\mathcal{G})
. Then by Lemma
47
, either
Q_{i}\to Q_{j}
, or
Q_{i}\leftarrow Q_{j}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. However both options,
Q_{i}\to Q_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{j-1}\to\dots\to Q_{i}
or
Q_{j}\to Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{i+1}\to\dots\to Q_{j}
contradict that
\mathcal{G}
is an ancestral graph.

(ii)

Suppose for a contradiction that every consecutive triple on
p
is shielded. Then by Lemma
47
it follows that
Q_{0}\leftarrow Q_{2}
and
Q_{k-1}\to Q_{k+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. If
k=2
, we then immediately reach a contradiction with
(i)
above.

Otherwise, suppose
k>2
.
Since
Q_{1}\leftrightarrow Q_{2}\leftrightarrow Q_{3}
is a shielded triple, it follows that
Q_{1}\leftarrow Q_{3}
or
Q_{1}\to Q_{3}
is in
\mathcal{G}
(Lemma
47
). However, since
Q_{0}\leftarrow Q_{2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}
, by
(i)
above,
we conclude that
Q_{1}\leftarrow Q_{3}
must be in
\mathcal{G}
. In fact, we can apply this argument iteratively to the remaining consecutive triples on
p
, until we reach
p(Q_{k-2},Q_{k+1})
contradicting
(i)
above.

(iii)
,
(iv)

Both of these cases follow from Lemma
4
.

Lemma 49
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths. Furthermore, suppose that the edge orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under rules
R1
-
R3
, and
Zhao-R4
. Suppose that there is a minimal collider path
p=\langle A=Q_{l_{k}},\dots,Q_{l_{1}},Q=Q_{r_{0}},Q_{r_{1}},\dots,Q_{r_{m}}=B\rangle
,
m\geq 1
,
k>1
in
\mathcal{G}
, and a node
W
that is not on
p
such that the following are in
\mathcal{G}
:

(a)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
, and

(b)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
, and

(c)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{i}}
, or
W\leftarrow Q_{l_{i}}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
,
1\leq k_{1}\leq k-2
and

(d)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
.

Then

(i)

Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G})
, and

(ii)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}
is not in
\mathcal{G}
.

Since
Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
44
implies that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G})
. So it only remains to show that
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}
is not in
\mathcal{G}
.
Suppose for a contradiction that
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Below we obtain a contradiction if
(1)

k_{1}>1
, and
(2)

k_{1}=1
.

(1)

Suppose first that
k_{1}>1
. If
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
together with Lemmas
44
and
47
implies that
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. By the same reasoning
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
implies that
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, for
i\in\{2,\dots,k_{1}\}
. However now,
p(Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}},Q)
contradicts
(i)
of Lemma
48
.

Otherwise,
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. But in this case,

Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}

together with Lemmas
44
and
47
implies that
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. By the same reasoning
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
implies that
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, for
i\in\{2,\dots,k_{1}+1\}
. However now,
p(Q_{l_{k_{1}+2}},Q)
contradicts
(i)
of Lemma
48
.

(2)

Next, consider the case when
k_{1}=1
. Since having
Q\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{1}}\to W
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
in
\mathcal{G}
would contradict that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R2
, we must have that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, since
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{3}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemmas
44
and
47
imply that
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{l_{3}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
.

If
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{2}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemmas
44
and

would lead us to conclude that
Q_{l_{2}}\to Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, making

contradict

of Lemma
48
.
Alternatively, if
Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then

Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
,

, and
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, together with

and Lemma

,
would imply that either

, or

are in
\mathcal{G}
. Having both

and

in

, would make

contradict

of Lemma

. Alternatively, having both

and
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
in
\mathcal{G}
, would make
p(Q_{l_{2}},Q_{r_{1}})
contradict
(i)
of Lemma
48
.

Lemma 50
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths and such that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
-
R3
,
Zhao-R4
. Suppose that there is a minimal collider path
p=\langle A=Q_{l_{k}},\dots,Q_{l_{1}},Q=Q_{r_{0}},Q_{r_{1}},\dots,Q_{r_{m}}=B\rangle
,
m\geq 1
,
k>1
, in
\mathcal{G}
, and a node
W
not on
p
such that

(a)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
, and

(b)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
, and

(c)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{i}}
, or
W\leftarrow Q_{l_{i}}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
,
k_{1}<k
and

(d)

Q_{l_{i}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
,
k_{1}<k
.

Then

(i)

\langle Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}},W\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}
, but not of the form
W\to Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}
, and

(ii)

Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

This proof is split into three cases depending on the forms of edges
\langle Q_{l_{k_{1}}},W\rangle
and
\langle W,Q\rangle
:
(a)

Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
,
(b)

Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
are in
\mathcal{G}
, and
(c)

Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
are in
\mathcal{G}
.

(a)

In this case we assume that
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Let
i_{1}\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
be the largest index such that
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. If such an index does not exist then let
i_{1}=0
and
Q_{l_{0}}=Q
since
Q_{l_{0}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

Since
k>k_{1},
we now have that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore,
Q_{l_{i^{\prime}}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
for all
i^{\prime}\in\{i_{1}+1,\dots,k_{1}\}
. Hence, since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
Zhao-R4
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G})
. Furthermore,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\leftarrow W
is not in
\mathcal{G}
since
\mathcal{G}
is ancestral. In fact, since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R2
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
Now,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
implies that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, by Lemmas
44
and
47
.

(b)

In this case we assume that
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
are in
\mathcal{G}.

Since
k>k_{1}
, and
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
44
implies that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G})
and that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\leftarrow W
is not in
\mathcal{G}.

Note also that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
is not possible, since
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
would by Lemmas
44
and
47
imply that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\leftarrow Q
thus, together with
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
, making
p(Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}},Q_{r_{1}})
contradict
(i)
of Lemma
48
.
Hence,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
implying that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is also in
\mathcal{G}
by Lemmas
44
and
47
.

(c)

n this case we assume that
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
are in
\mathcal{G}.
Let
Q_{l_{0}}=Q
. As in the above cases, note that since
k>k_{1}
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore, Lemma
44
implies that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G})
and
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\leftarrow W
is not in
\mathcal{G}.
If
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G},
we can use exactly the same argument as in
(b)
to show that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}.

Otherwise,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Suppose first that
k_{1}=1
. Then
Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
,
Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{1}}\leftrightarrow Q
, and
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, together with
R3
and Lemma
47
, imply that
Q_{l_{2}}\to Q
, or
Q_{l_{2}}\leftarrow Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Since
Q_{l_{2}}\leftarrow Q
together with
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
would imply that
p(Q_{l_{2}},Q_{r_{1}})
contradicts
(i)
of Lemma
48
, it must be that
Q_{l_{2}}\to Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
. We can now apply

and Lemma
47
to
Q_{l_{2}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
,
Q_{l_{2}}\to Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
, and
Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
to conclude that
Q_{l_{2}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
must be in
\mathcal{G}.

Next, suppose that
k_{1}>1
.
Note, that if there is any edge
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
, or
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\to W
in
\mathcal{G}
, for
i_{1}\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}-1\}
, we can construct a contradiction with Lemma
49
. Hence, all edges
\langle Q_{l_{i_{1}}},W\rangle,i_{1}\in\{0,\dots,k_{1}-1\}
must be of the form
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Note that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}-1}}
and
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
with
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{k_{1}-1}}
and
R3
imply that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\in\Adj(Q_{l_{k_{1}-1}},\mathcal{G})
. Due to Lemmas
47
, and
48
, this edge must be of the form
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{l_{k_{1}-1}}
.

Then
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}-2}}
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
, and
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{l_{k_{1}-1}}\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{k_{1}-2}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, by
R3
, Lemma
47
, and Lemma
48
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{l_{k_{1}-2}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Since
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
for all
i_{1}\in\{0,\dots,k_{1}\}
, we can keep iterating the above procedure until we get that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
. The conclusion that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then follows from the above paragraph.

Lemma 51
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths and such that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
-
R3
,
Zhao-R4
. Suppose that there is a minimal collider path
p=\langle A=Q_{l_{k}},\dots,Q_{l_{1}},Q=Q_{r_{0}},Q_{r_{1}},\dots,Q_{r_{m}}=B\rangle
,
k,m,\geq 1
,
m+k\geq 2
in
\mathcal{G}
, and a node
W
that is not on
p
such that
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then edges
\langle Q_{l_{1}},W\rangle
, and
\langle W,Q_{r_{1}}\rangle
are in
\mathcal{G}
. Furthermore, both of these edges are into
W
.

First note that edges
\langle Q_{l_{1}},W\rangle
, and
\langle W,Q_{r_{1}}\rangle
are in
\mathcal{G}
by Lemma
44
. Furthermore, by the same lemma, neither
W\to Q_{l_{1}}
, nor
W\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, we have the following options for the triple
\langle Q_{l_{1}},W,Q_{r_{1}}\rangle
,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
. For the remainder of the proof, our goal is to rule out the latter three options.

Note that if
k=1
, we can rule out that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, since in this case Lemma
44
, would imply that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, but since
Q_{l_{1}}=A
that would contradict that
p
is a minimal collider path.
Similarly, if
m=1
, we can rule out that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, by a symmetric argument.
Furthermore, if
k=m=1
, we can also rule out that
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
, since that in combination with
Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, and
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\notin\Adj(Q_{r_{1}},\mathcal{G})
contradicts that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R3
. Hence, if
k=m=1
, we are done.

For the rest of the proof, suppose that either
k>1
or
m>1
and for contradiction suppose that one of the following is in
\mathcal{G}
:
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
, or
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
.
Note also that if
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, by Lemmas
44
and
47
, so either
k>1
or we have reached a contradiction with
p
being a minimal collider path. Similarly if
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
then
Q_{l_{1}}\leftarrow Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, by Lemmas
44
and
47
, so either
m>1
or we have reached a contradiction with
p
being a minimal collider path.
Lastly, if
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
and
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
are also in
\mathcal{G}
and since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R3
,
Q_{l_{1}}\in\Adj(Q_{r_{1}},\mathcal{G})
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
By Lemma
47
,
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
, or
Q_{l_{1}}\leftarrow Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Note that if
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
then either
k>1
, or we have reached a contradiction with
p
being a minimal collider path, and similarly, if
Q_{l_{1}}\leftarrow Q_{r_{1}}
then either
m>1
, or we have reached a contradiction with
p
being a minimal collider path.
Therefore, the following combinations remain to be discussed:

(a)

k>1
, and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
, or

(b)

m>1
, and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\leftarrow Q_{r_{1}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
.

The proof for the above cases is symmetric, so without loss of generality we will assume that
k>1
,
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
and
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
are in
\mathcal{G}
and show that assumption leads to a contradiction.
We will show a contradiction under the following assumptions:
(1)
there is no
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and
(2)
there exists an
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

(1)

There is no
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. In this case,
Q_{l_{1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, or
Q_{l_{1}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
and by assumption
Q_{l_{1}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
and
W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{r_{1}}
are also in
\mathcal{G}
. We can now use Lemma
50
iteratively to show that
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, or
Q_{l_{i}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Additionally, by the same lemma, we will also have that
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. Since
Q_{l_{k}}=A
, we now reach a contradiction with Lemma
47
.

(2)

There is an
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}
is the closest such node to
Q
on
p(A,Q)
. In this case,
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
and
Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
or
Q_{l_{i}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,i_{1}-1\}
. Furthermore, by Lemma
50
,
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,i_{1}\}
. Since
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{i_{1}-1}}\to W
, or
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\leftrightarrow Q_{l_{i_{1}-1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
, by the ancestral property of
\mathcal{G}
and Lemma
44
,
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\leftarrow W
is not in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is either
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\leftrightarrow W
or
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}W
.

Now, since
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\to Q_{r_{1}}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, either
i_{1}=k
and we have reached a contradiction with Lemma
47
, or by Lemma
48
, there is a node
Q_{l_{i_{2}}}
on
p(A,Q_{l_{i_{1}}})
such that
Q_{l_{i_{2}}}\notin\Adj(Q_{r_{1}},\mathcal{G})
, and
Q_{l_{i}}\to Q_{r_{1}},
for all
i\in\{i_{1},\dots,i_{2}-1\}
.
But in this case, we also have the path
p(Q_{l_{i_{2}}},Q_{l_{i_{1}}})\oplus\langle Q_{l_{i_{1}}},W\rangle\oplus\langle W,Q_{r_{1}}\rangle
which contradicts that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
Zhao-R4
.

Lemma 52
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph that does not contain inducing paths and such that orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
-
R3
,
Zhao-R4
. Suppose that there is a minimal collider path
p=\langle A=Q_{l_{k}},\dots,Q_{l_{1}},Q,Q_{r_{1}},\dots,Q_{r_{m}}=B\rangle
,
m\geq 1
,
k>1
, in
\mathcal{G}
and a node
W
not on
p
such that

(a)

W\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and

(b)

Q_{l_{i}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
or
Q_{l_{i}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
for
i\in\{1,\dots,k_{1}\}
,
k_{1}<k
.

Then
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

Suppose first that
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then directly by Lemma
51
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
Hence, for the remainder suppose that
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\to W
and let
Q_{l_{0}}\equiv Q
.
Let
i_{1}\in\{0,\dots,k_{1}-1\}
be such that
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}
is the closest node to
Q_{l_{k_{1}}}
on
p(Q_{l_{k_{1}}},Q)
such that
Q_{l_{i_{1}}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}W
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Now,
Q_{l_{i}}\to W
for all
i\in\{i_{1}+1,\dots,k_{1}\}
and
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow Q
is in
\mathcal{G}
, so since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
Zhao-R4
, it follows that
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\in\Adj(W,\mathcal{G}).
Since
\mathcal{G}
is ancestral and
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{l_{k_{1}}}\to W
is in
\mathcal{G}
,
Q_{l_{k_{1}+1}}\leftarrow W
is not in
\mathcal{G}
. Additionally, since orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R2
,

Appendix D
Supplement to Section
5

We prove the theorem by contradiction while considering different possibilities for the orientation of the
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.3,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.8,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
edge. Hence, suppose for a contradiction that there is a MAG
\mathcal{M}
represented by
\mathcal{G}
that contains
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
and
(i)

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
, or
(ii)

A\to B
.

(i)

We immediately have the contradiction in this case, as
D\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is an unshielded collider in
\mathcal{M}
that is not in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
\mathcal{M}
cannot be represented by
\mathcal{G}
.

(ii)

We assume that
A\to B
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
are in
\mathcal{M}
. Then
D\to A
cannot be in
\mathcal{M}
, as
C\to D\to A\to B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is either a directed or an almost directed cycle. Hence,
D\leftrightarrow A
is in
\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, using similar reasoning,
C\to D\leftrightarrow A\to B
implies that
B\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{M}
, and
C\to D\leftrightarrow A
implies that
A\leftrightarrow C
. But this gives us an inducing path
D\leftrightarrow A\leftrightarrow C\leftrightarrow B
in
\mathcal{M}
, which is a contradiction.

Suppose for a contradiction that there exists a MAG
\mathcal{M}
represented by
\mathcal{G}
such that
V_{1}\to V_{2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
and let
p=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{i}\rangle
, and
q=\langle V_{i},V_{i+1},V_{1}\rangle.
.

Since
\mathcal{M}
contains only those unshielded colliders already present in
\mathcal{G}
and since
p
is an unshielded possibly directed path in
\mathcal{G}
, we will have that the path corresponding to
p
in
\mathcal{M}
is of the form
V_{1}\to V_{2}\to\dots\to V_{i}
. Hence, the paths corresponding to
p
and
q
in
\mathcal{M}
an almost directed cycle, which is a contradiction with
\mathcal{M}
being an ancestral graph.

Suppose for a contradiction that there is a MAG
\mathcal{M}
represented by
\mathcal{G}
that contains
A\to B
.
Since
\mathcal{M}
does not contain new unshielded colliders compared to
\mathcal{G}
, the paths corresponding to
\langle A,B,\dots,V_{i}\rangle
, must be of the form
A\to B\to\dots\to V_{i}
in
\mathcal{M}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.
Furthermore, since
\mathcal{M}
is ancestral, the path
C\leftrightarrow A\to\dots\to\dots V_{1}
in
\mathcal{M}
implies that the edge
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{1}
in
\mathcal{G}
is oriented as
C\leftrightarrow V_{1}
in
\mathcal{M}
. Similarly, path
D\leftrightarrow A\to\dots\to V_{k}
in
\mathcal{M}
implies
D\leftrightarrow V_{k}
is in
\mathcal{M}
. However, now, any orientation of the remaining edges on unshielded path
\langle C,V_{1},\dots,V_{k},D\rangle
implies a presence of a new unshielded collider in
\mathcal{M}
compared to
\mathcal{G}
, which is a contradiction.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that there is a MAG
\mathcal{M}
represented by
\mathcal{G}
that contains
Q_{k}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
. Let
p_{\mathcal{M}}
be the path in
\mathcal{M}
corresponding to

in
\mathcal{G}
.
Note that
p
is not a collider path. Moreover, there cannot be a subsequence of

that forms a collider path in
\mathcal{G}
since that would require an edge of the form

and by choice of

there is no such edge in
\mathcal{G}
.

We will derive the contradiction by proving that there is a subsequence of

that forms a collider path from

to

in
\mathcal{M}.
Hence, there is also a subsequence of

that forms a minimal collider path from

to

, which ultimately gives us the contradiction with

being represented by
\mathcal{G}
by Definition
5
.

Note that since

is a collider on

.
If the remaining non-endpoint nodes on

are colliders, then the contradiction is immediate.
Otherwise, there is at least one non-endpoint node on

that is a non-collider. Let

Hence, let

. Since
