Figure 15
:
Proof structure of Theorem
30

Figure
15
displays how the supporting results come together to prove Theorem
30
.

Consider the following procedure.
First, we identify the circle component of
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
. This is the subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
containing only
edges,
\mathbf{E}_{C}
. Call this
\mathcal{G}_{C}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E}_{C})
. Consider the same edges present in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
, which might potentially have different edge mark orientations,
\mathbf{E}_{C}^{\prime}
.
Note that by Lemma
36
,
\mathcal{G}_{C}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E}_{C})
is a collection of undirected connected chordal components
\mathcal{G}_{C_{1}},\dots\mathcal{G}_{C_{k}}
,
k\geq 1
, each of which is an induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
.
We will refer to the corresponding induced subgraphs of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{G}_{C_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,\mathcal{G}_{C_{k}}^{\prime}
. Theorem
29
tells us that each individual induced subgraph
\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}^{\prime}
,
i\geq 1
of
\mathcal{G}_{C}^{\prime}
is a restricted essential ancestral graph. That is, each
\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}^{\prime}
can be oriented into a MAG
\mathcal{M}_{i}
with no minimal collider paths, and with the desired edge orientation of a particular edge
\langle A,B\rangle
.

Now, suppose we construct a new directed mixed graph
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{M}})
obtained by taking the union of all invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. We will now show that
\mathcal{M}
is a MAG represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. That is
\mathcal{M}
is an ancestral graph with the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(Lemma
27
).
In particular, it suffices to show that there are no directed cycles or almost directed cycles in
\mathcal{M}
that contain some edges from
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and some edges from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that are not in any
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, and also that there are no minimal collider paths in
\mathcal{M}
that are made up of edges from
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and edges outside of
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
that are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

First, we show that
\mathcal{M}
is ancestral.
By Lemma
24
, it is enough to show that there are no directed cycles or almost directed cycles of length 3 in
\mathcal{M}
. For sake of contradiction, we will suppose that there is a triple
A\to B\to C
and edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
in
\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, for all
i
are ancestral, and since
\mathcal{G}_{C_{i}}^{\prime}
are induced subgraphs of
\mathcal{G}_{C}^{\prime}
(Lemma
36
)
\forall i
, exactly two of the nodes
A,B,C
are in
\mathcal{G}_{C_{j}}^{\prime}
for some
j\geq 1
. We consider the options below:

(a)

Suppose that
A,C
are in
\mathcal{G}_{C_{j}}^{\prime},
and
B\notin\mathcal{G}_{C}
. Note again that
A\to B\to C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, since
A,C\in\mathcal{G}_{C_{j}}^{\prime}
and
B\notin\mathcal{G}_{C}
, we have that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and also that
B\to C
or
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Now Lemma
40
, implies that
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}A
must have been in
\mathcal{G}
, which leads us to a contradiction.

(b)

Suppose that
A,B
are in
\mathcal{G}_{C_{j}}^{\prime},
and
C\notin\mathcal{G}_{C}
. Again, consider that
A\to B\to C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
are in
\mathcal{M}
. Therefore, similarly to above, we have that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
and since
C\notin\mathcal{G}_{C}
,
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence, we obtain a contradiction with Lemma
40
as in the previous case.

(c)

Suppose that
B,C
are in
\mathcal{G}_{C_{j}}^{\prime},
and
A\notin\mathcal{G}_{C}
. Now again
A\to B\to C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
are in
\mathcal{M}
. Now,
C\to A\to B
or
C\leftrightarrow A\to B
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. So since edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R2
, the edge
\langle C,B\rangle
must have an arrowhead at
B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. But, this contradicts that
B\to C
is in
\mathcal{M}
.

Therefore,
\mathcal{M}
is ancestral.
It remains to prove that
\mathcal{M}
has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Suppose for a contradiction, there is a minimal collider path
p_{\mathcal{M}}=\langle V_{1},\dots,V_{r}\rangle
,
r\geq 3
in
\mathcal{M}
such that the corresponding path
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is not a collider path. Furthermore, we will choose the shortest such path
p_{\mathcal{M}}
and denote the corresponding paths (same sequences of nodes) in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
and in
\mathcal{G}
as
p_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Since there are no minimal collider paths in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1\dots,k\}
, and since a node in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is not in
\mathcal{M}_{j}
, for
i\neq j
,
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we know that at least one edge on
p_{\mathcal{M}}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
. Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
contains exactly the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}
, there is also at least one edge mark on
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that is in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1\dots,k\}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Note first that
p_{\mathcal{M}}
cannot be an unshielded collider itself, and that
p_{\mathcal{M}}
cannot contain an unshielded collider that is not on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
. This is because none of the
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
i\in\{1\dots,k\}
, graphs contain unshielded colliders, and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
itself does not contain unshielded collider that are not already in
\mathcal{G}
.
Furthermore, we cannot have a path
\langle A,B,C\rangle
in
\mathcal{M}
, where
\langle A,B\rangle
is in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, and
\langle B,C\rangle
is in
\mathcal{M}_{j}
, where
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, and
i\neq j
(due to Lemma
36
).
Furthermore, we know that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
does not contain any unshielded collider
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
, where
\langle A,B\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, and
\langle B,C\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, or vice versa (based on Lemma
40
the fact that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
does not contain new unshielded colliders compared to
\mathcal{G}
) and also that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
also cannot contain
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
, where
A\notin\Adj(C,\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
, due to orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
being closed under
R1
.

Hence, any consecutive triple of nodes on
p_{\mathcal{M}}
is either shielded, or the corresponding triple is already an unshielded collider on
p_{\mathcal{G}}
. In particular, any triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
,
l\in\{1,\dots,r-2\}
on
p_{\mathcal{M}}
such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
is shielded.

Since
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
is not a collider path we now consider the following options for choosing a triple on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
which will be used to derive our desired contradiction.

(a)

Choose a triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
, with the smallest index
l\in\{1,\dots,r-2\}
on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
that is of one of the following forms in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
:

(a1)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\in\Adj(V_{l+2},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and
such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, or

(a2)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, or

(a3)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
.

(b)

Choose a triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
, with the largest index
l\in\{1,\dots,r-2\}
on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
, that is of one of the following forms in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
:

(b1)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
V_{l}\in\Adj(V_{l+2},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some

, and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in

but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, or

(b2)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some

and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, or

(b3)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and such that
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and

is in

for some

.

Note that cases
(a)
and
(b)
cover all options for the form of the triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}},
so we are assured that one of the above options will exist on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}.
Also, note that case
(b)
is symmetric to case
(a)
, and the proof will be using exactly the same arguments Hence, without loss of generality, we only derive a contradiction for cases
(a)
.

(a)

We discuss all three possible forms of the triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
below and derive a contradiction in each case.

(a)(a1)
or
(a)(a2)

In this case we assume that either:

•

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
V_{l}\in\Adj(V_{l+2},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and moreover,
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

•

Or that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
and
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and
moreover,
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Hence, consider the form of edge
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}
. If this edge is of the form
V_{l}\to V_{l+1}
,
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1},
or
V_{l}\leftrightarrow V_{l+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
, then Lemma
66
tells us that the form of the edge
\langle V_{l},V_{l+2}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
would allow us construct a shorter minimal collider path than
p_{\mathcal{M}}
by skipping over
V_{l+1}
, which leads us to a contradiction.

Next, we consider the case where
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is of the form
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
. Then Lemma
66
implies that
V_{l}\to V_{l+2}
or
V_{l}\leftrightarrow V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
. In the latter case, we again get a contradiction with
p_{\mathcal{M}}
being a minimal collider path, as we could replace
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
with
\langle V_{l},V_{l+2}\rangle
. Similarly, we get the same contradiction if
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is the first edge on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
and
p_{\mathcal{M}}
, regardless of the form of the
\langle V_{l},V_{l+2}\rangle
edge in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

Hence, suppose that
V_{l}\to V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
and that
l>1
, meaning that
V_{l}\leftrightarrow V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
(corresponding to
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
).
Next, note that if
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(V_{1},V_{l+1})
is of the form
V_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow V_{l+1}
, case
(iv)
of Lemma
66
would imply that we can choose a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
as a shorter minimal collider path, which is a contradiction. Otherwise, there is at least one edge
\langle V_{j},V_{j+1}\rangle
,
1\leq j<l
on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(V_{1},V_{l+1})
that corresponds to
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
, and also by case
(iv)
of Lemma
66
, there are edges
V_{i}\to V_{l+2}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
for every
j+1<i\leq l
, and also that
V_{j+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
or
V_{j+1}\to V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Let
\langle V_{j},V_{j+1}\rangle
,
1\leq j<l
be indeed such an edge on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(V_{1},V_{l+1})
chosen so that the index
j
is the largest possible.

Now, consider the triple
\langle V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. By choice of our original triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
, we can conclude that the triple
\langle V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
must be of one of the forms in
(b)
, and more precisely, either of the form described in case
(b)(b1)
or case
(b)(b2)
.

In either case, we have that either
V_{j}\leftrightarrow V_{j+2}
or
V_{j}\leftarrow V_{j+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by Lemma
66
. If
V_{j}\leftrightarrow V_{j+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we obtain our desired contradiction by constructing a shorter collider path
p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},V_{j})\oplus\langle V_{j},V_{j+2}\rangle\oplus p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{j+2},V_{r})
.
If
V_{j}\leftarrow V_{j+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, then we must be in case
(iv)
of Lemma
66
, so that
V_{j}\leftarrow V_{s}
, or
V_{j}\leftrightarrow V_{s}
,
j+2\leq s\leq l
and either
V_{j}\leftarrow V_{l+1}
,
V_{j}\leftrightarrow V_{l+1},
or
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}.
If any of the mentioned edges is of the form
\leftrightarrow
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we obtain a contradiction. Otherwise, we consider the edges between the following nodes in
\mathcal{G}
:
V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}
.

We know that
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
and
V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. We also know that
V_{l+1}\to V_{j}
or
V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{j}
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and that similarly
V_{j+1}\to V_{l+2}
or
V_{j+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

If
V_{l+1}\to V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
or
V_{l+1}\to V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then Lemma
40
and completeness of

in
\mathcal{G}

imply that
V_{l+1}\to V_{j+1}
or
V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Similarly, if
V_{j+1}\to V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
or
V_{j+1}\to V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
are in
\mathcal{G}
, then Lemmas
40
and completeness of
R2
in
\mathcal{G}

imply that
V_{j+1}\to V_{l+1}
or
V_{j+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
Both of these cannot be true at the same time, so at least one of the edges
\langle V_{l+1},V_{j}\rangle
or
\langle V_{j+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
are of the form
in
\mathcal{G}.

Furthermore, if
V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then the edge
\langle V_{l+2},V_{j+1}\rangle
must also be of the form
in
\mathcal{G}
(Lemma
38
). Analogously, if
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{j+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1},
is in
\mathcal{G}
, we conclude that
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
as well.

Hence, now we have an undirected cycle of length
4
in
\mathcal{G}
. Then by the chordal property of the circle component of
\mathcal{G}
(Lemma
36
), either
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
or
V_{j+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Let us assume without loss of generality that
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and consider the form of this edge in
\mathcal{M}
.
If
V_{j}\to V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
, then this edge together with
V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\to V_{j}
contradicts that
\mathcal{M}
is ancestral. If
V_{j}\leftarrow V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
, then this edge together with
V_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{j+1}\to V_{l+2}
contradicts that
\mathcal{M}
is ancestral.
Hence, the only option is for
V_{j}\leftrightarrow V_{l+2}
to be in
\mathcal{M}
, in which case
p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},V_{j})\oplus\langle V_{j},V_{l+2}\rangle\oplus p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{l+2},V_{r})
is a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
in
\mathcal{M}
that forms a shorter collider path, which is a contradiction.

(a)(a3)

V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
such that
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+2}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
\langle V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
. By Lemma
67
, we have that either
V_{l}\leftrightarrow V_{l+2}
or
V_{l}\to V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. In the former case, we again get a contradiction with
p_{\mathcal{M}}
being a minimal collider path, as we could replace
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
with
\langle V_{l},V_{l+2}\rangle
. Similarly, we get the same contradiction if
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1}\rangle
is the first edge on
p_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
and
p_{\mathcal{M}}
, regardless of the form of the
\langle V_{l},V_{l+2}\rangle
edge in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
.

Hence, suppose that
V_{l}\to V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
and that
l>1
, meaning that
V_{l}\leftrightarrow V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{M}
(corresponding to
V_{l}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
in
\mathcal{G}
).
Suppose first that
V_{l-1}
is also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}.

Since
V_{l+2}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, if
V_{l+2}\notin\Adj(V_{l-1},\mathcal{G})
, we have that
V_{l+1}\to V_{l-1}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by
R1
, and therefore,
\langle V_{l+2},V_{l+1},V_{l},V_{l-1}\rangle
would be a minimal discriminating collider path for
V_{l}
that is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
but not in
\mathcal{G}
, therefore giving us our contradiction.

Otherwise,
V_{l+2}\in\Adj(V_{l-1},\mathcal{G})
. In this case consider again the edge
\langle V_{l-1},V_{l+1}\rangle
in
\mathcal{M}
. If
V_{l-1}\leftrightarrow V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
we obtain a contradiction with our choice of path. If
V_{l-1}\leftarrow V_{l+1},
then due to ancestrality of
\mathcal{M}
, we have that
V_{l-1}\leftrightarrow V_{l+2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
, then again there is a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that forms a collider path in
\mathcal{M}
which also gives us a contradiction.

Otherwise,
V_{l+2}\in\Adj(V_{l-1},\mathcal{G})
and
V_{l-1}\to V_{l+1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
. Let
j
be chosen as the smallest index on
p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},V_{l-1})
such that
V_{j},\dots,V_{l},V_{l+1}
are all in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
. Then all of the nodes in
V_{j},\dots,V_{l+1}
must be in the same clique since we do not create any minimal collider paths in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Furthermore, if any edge
V_{d}\leftrightarrow V_{s}
,
j\leq d<d+1<s\leq l+1
is in
\mathcal{M}
, we can choose a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that is a shorter collider path. Moreover, since
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}
are ancestral, it follows that either
V_{d}\to V_{s}
for all pairs
j\leq d<d+1<s\leq l+1
or
V_{d}\to V_{s}
. If
j=1
, we now have that
\langle V_{1},V_{l+1}\rangle\oplus p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{l+1},V_{r})
is a collider path, which is a contradiction.

Otherwise,
j\neq 1
, and consider the triple
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1}\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}.
Note that
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j}\rangle
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, otherwise it would be in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}
(Lemma
36
). Hence,
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, and
\langle V_{j},V_{j+1}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C_{i}}.

By choice of our original triple
\langle V_{l},V_{l+1},V_{l+2}\rangle
, we can conclude that the triple
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1}\rangle
must be of the form in case
(b)(b3)
, that is
V_{j-1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
.

If
V_{j-1}\leftrightarrow V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
, then of course,
p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},V_{j-1})\oplus\langle V_{j-1},V_{j+1}\rangle\oplus p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{j+1},V_{r})
is a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that forms a minimal collider path and give us our contradiction.

Otherwise,
V_{j-1}\leftarrow V_{j+1}
is in
\mathcal{M}
and we focus on the subpath
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
.
Since
V_{j}\to V_{j+2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
by assumption, we have that if edge
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j+2}\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then due to the ancestral property of
\mathcal{M}
,
V_{j-1}\leftrightarrow V_{j+2}
is in
\mathcal{M}
and then similarly to above,
p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{1},V_{j-1})\oplus\langle V_{j-1},V_{j+2}\rangle\oplus p_{\mathcal{M}}(V_{j+2},V_{r})
is a subsequence of
p_{\mathcal{M}}
that forms a minimal collider path and give us our contradiction. If however
V_{j-1}\notin\Adj(V_{j+2},\mathcal{G})
, then consider that
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
is an inducing path and a minimal collider path in
\mathcal{M}
. Since
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
is an inducing path in
\mathcal{M}
, this path cannot be collider path in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(otherwise, it would be a possibly inducing path and contradict Lemma
27
). Furthermore, since
1\leq j\leq l-1<r
,
\langle V_{j-1},V_{j},V_{j+1},V_{j+2}\rangle
is shorter than
p_{\mathcal{M}}
, so our choice of a minimal collider path is incorrect and we obtain our contradiction.

E.1.1
Supporting Results for Theorem
30

Lemma 66
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V,E^{\prime}})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph and
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an essential ancestral graph such that
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same skeleton, the same set of minimal collider paths, and all invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}
exist and are identical in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Suppose furthermore, that all
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
edges in
\mathcal{G}
correspond to
A\to B
or
A\leftrightarrow B
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and that orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
. Suppose that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, where this edge is of one of the following forms in
\mathcal{G}
:
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
,
E\to C
,
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
E\leftrightarrow C
. Furthermore, suppose that there is an edge
\langle C,D\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that corresponds to
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}
, and also suppose that edge
\langle E,D\rangle
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(1)

If the form of the edge
\langle C,D\rangle
is
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, or

(2)

if the form of the edge
\langle C,D\rangle
is
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, while the form of the edge
\langle E,D\rangle
is
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
,

then the following hold:

(i)

If
E\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and also
E\to D
or
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and also
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and also
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then either

•

E\to C
and
E\to D
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, or

•

E\to C
and
E\leftrightarrow D
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, or

•

E\leftrightarrow C
and
E\leftrightarrow D
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, or

•

E\to C
and
E\leftrightarrow D
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, in this setting, we have that

(a)

for every
P_{1}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
P_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}E
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime},

P_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and

(b)

for every
P_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}P_{2}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow P_{k}
,
P_{k}\equiv E
,
k>1

either there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
P_{i}\leftrightarrow D
and
P_{j}\to D
, for all
j\in\{i+1,\dots k\}
or
P_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
and
P_{i}\to D
for all
i\in\{2,\dots,k\}
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(i)

Since
E\to C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40
implies that
E\to D
or
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
are in
\mathcal{G}
. Since all
[formula]
edges in
\mathcal{G}
correspond to
\to
or
\leftrightarrow
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we know that
E\to D
, or
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

Since
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and
E\in\Adj(D,\mathcal{G})
, we have by Lemmas
40

and the fact orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R2
, that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
or
E\leftarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then
E\leftrightarrow D
or
E\leftarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

In case
(2)
, we then immediately have that
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Now, in case
(1)
,
E\leftrightarrow C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and the fact that orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed with respect to
R2
would imply that
E\leftarrow D
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
and therefore,
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40
and completeness of
R2
in
\mathcal{G}
imply that
E\leftrightarrow D
is also in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
E\leftrightarrow C
and
E\leftrightarrow D
are also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40

implies
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
or
E\to D
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Then we have the combination of cases as listed above. In particular, if
E\leftrightarrow C
and
E\to D
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we also have that cases
(iv)a
and
(iv)b
hold because
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is ancestral and that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}
. Note that Lemma
28
unshielded collider in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is also an unshielded collider in
\mathcal{G}
and every collider discriminated by a path in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
must be a collider on the corresponding path in
\mathcal{G}
. Since we know that
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, we know that the paths of the form
P_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}P_{i+1}\leftrightarrow\dots\leftrightarrow P_{k}\leftrightarrow E\leftrightarrow C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
cannot be discriminating paths, hence
P_{i}\in\Adj(D,\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
. The rest of the argument follows by using completeness of orientation rules
R1
,
R2
, and
R4
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 67
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V,E^{\prime}})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph and
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an essential ancestral graph such that
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same skeleton, the same set of minimal collider paths, and all invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}
exist and are identical in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Suppose furthermore, that all
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
edges in
\mathcal{G}
correspond to
A\to B
or
A\leftrightarrow B
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and that orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
. Suppose that

is an edge in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that corresponds to
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}
, and also that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, where this edge is of one of the following forms in
\mathcal{G}
:
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
,
E\to C
,
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
E\leftrightarrow C
, then there is an edge
\langle E,D\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and suppose that this edge is of the form
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}.

Then

(i)

If
E\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and also
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then either
E\to C
or
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
E\leftrightarrow D
or
E\leftarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(i)

Since
E\to C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40
implies that
E\to D
or
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
are in
\mathcal{G}
. Since all
[formula]
edges in
\mathcal{G}
correspond to
\to
or
\leftrightarrow
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we know that
E\to D
, or
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. By assumption, we know
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and hence,
E\leftrightarrow D
must be in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
E\leftrightarrow C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40
and completeness of
R2
in
\mathcal{G}
, imply that
E\leftrightarrow D
is also in
\mathcal{G}
. Hence,
E\leftrightarrow C
and
E\leftrightarrow D
are also in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iii)

If
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then since
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
, Lemma
40

implies
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
or
E\to D
is in
\mathcal{G}
.
Since we know, that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, it must be that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}
and
E\leftrightarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

(iv)

Since
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{P}
, and
E\in\Adj(D,\mathcal{G})
, we have by Lemmas
40

and the fact that orientations under
R2
are closed in
\mathcal{G}
that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}D
or
E\leftarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Since we assume that
E\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, this implies that
E\leftrightarrow D
or
E\leftarrow D
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

E.2
Theorem
32

Consider constructing the graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
by replacing all edges
\langle S,T\rangle
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that are of the form
S\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}T
in both
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}
with
S\to T
.
By Theorem
68
,

\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
is ancestral, has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
,
R8
,
R11
The proof is now complete as
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy Theorem
30
.

Theorem 68
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph and
\mathcal{G}
be an essential ancestral graph such that
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same skeleton, the same set of minimal collider paths, and all invariant edge marks in
\mathcal{G}
exist and are identical in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Suppose furthermore, that every edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}
corresponds either to
A\to B
or to
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
,
R8
,
R11
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
be identical to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
except all
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
edges in
\mathcal{G}
correspond to
A\to B
edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
. Then edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
are closed under
R1
-
R4
,
R8
-
R13
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
is ancestral and has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

We first consider showing that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
are closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
,
R8
,
R11
. It is enough to consider each orientation rule and show that the antecedent for any rule will not occur in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
directly. A lot of the arguments below will use the fact that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
does not contain any new arrowhead edge marks compared to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are already closed under
R1
,
R2
,
R4
,
R8
,
R11
.

R1

The antecedent of
R1
requires a triple
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
to exist in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, and
A\notin\Adj(C,\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime})
. We know this type of triple cannot exist in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
because we do not introduce any arrowhead edge marks in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
compared to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R1
-
R4
,
R8
-
R13
.

R2

Having the antecedent of
R2
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
would require that there is a triple
A,B,C
in
\mathcal{G}
such that

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and in
\mathcal{G}
, and

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\to C
or
A\to B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, but

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and by assumption

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

Note that if either
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
are in
\mathcal{G}
, then
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}
by Lemma
40
.
Similarly, having either
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
in
\mathcal{G}
, together with edge
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
would imply a contradiction with Lemma
40
, as Lemma
40
would insist on an arrowhead at
A
on edge
\langle A,B\rangle
.

R4

The antecedent of
R4
would require the presence of:

•

an almost discriminating path
p=\langle A,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},Q_{k+1}=B,\rangle
for
Q_{k}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
,
A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G})
, with

•

Q_{k}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
also being in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Then
p(A,Q_{k})
is then an almost collider path in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, and by inspecting the definition of an almost collider path (Definition
15
), it is clear that

•

\langle A,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k}\rangle
must also be an almost collider path in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

However, since
\langle A,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{k},B\rangle
is not an almost discriminating path in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(otherwise,
Q_{k}\to B
would be in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
), at least one of the edges
\langle Q_{i},B\rangle
is of the form
Q_{i}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Note that since all edges
\langle Q_{i},B\rangle
,
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
are of the form
Q_{i}\to B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, the form of all of these edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is either
\to
or
[formula]
.
Let
Q_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
,
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
be an edge in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, chosen such that there is no edge of that form with a smaller index than
j
.

If
j=1
, then we know that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{1}
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, otherwise, edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
would not be closed under
R1
. Examining the definition of an almost collider path, we now know that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.1,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}Q_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{2}
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{2}
are in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
,
Furthermore,
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{2}
implies
Q_{2}\to Y
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by
R1
. Now consider the relationships between nodes
A,Q_{1},Q_{2}
and
B
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
:

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}Q_{2}\to B
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and so are

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}Q_{2}
, and

•

Q_{1}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
, and in addition,

•

A\notin\Adj(B,\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
.

Now, the above implies that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are not closed under
R11
, which is a contradiction.

Next, suppose that
j>1
and
Q_{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Now, by definition of the almost discriminating path we have that
\langle A,Q_{1},\dots,Q_{j}\rangle\oplus\langle Q_{j},B\rangle
is an almost discriminating path for
Q_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. However, this now implies that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are not closed under
R4
, which is a contradiction.

R8

Having the antecedent of
R8
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
but not in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
would require that there is a triple
A,B,C
in
\mathcal{G}
such that

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, and

•

A\to B\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, but

•

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\to C
, or
A\to B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
, or
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Also, note that since
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, it must be that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
is in
\mathcal{G}
. Lemma
40
then implies that
B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
cannot be in
\mathcal{G}
. Meaning that the only option among the above listed is to have
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
,
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.0}}\put(4.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
in
\mathcal{G}
. However, this option also contradicts Lemma
40
.

R11

For the antecedent of
R11
consider the left panel of Figure
4
. To have this graph as an induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, but not of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, edge
C\to D
must have been
C\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}D
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. However, this would contradict that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are under
R1
.

Next we show that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
is ancestral and has the same minimal collider paths as
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. The latter follows immediately since we do not introduce any arrowheads in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
, or remove edges compared to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Suppose for a contradiction that there is a directed or almost directed cycle in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
. By Lemma
24
, there is also one such cycle of length
3
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
. Let
A\to B\to C
,
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
be one such cycle in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is ancestral, we know that the corresponding edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are in one of the following categories:

(a)

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\to C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
.

(b)

A\to B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}C
.

(c)

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
and
A\leftarrow C
.

(d)

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
and
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(4.3,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\end{picture}C
.

(e)

A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.2,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}C
and
A\leftrightarrow C
.

Note that cases
(a)
-
(c)
contradict that edge mark orientations in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are closed under
R2
and
R8
.
The edges in case
(d)
-
(e)
must be of that same form in
\mathcal{G}
. However,
(d)
contradicts Lemma
41
and
(e)
contradicts Lemma
40
.

E.3
Additional Simulations

A key assumption in our work is that the orientation knowledge we add is consistent with at least the essential ancestral graph, if not the true underlying MAG. In practice, we can learn the essential ancestral graph only from finite samples, and therefore its correctness is not always correct. Therefore, the orientation knowledge may not be consistent with the learned essential graph even if it is consistent with the true MAG. In these scenarios, Algorithm
2
will fail.

We perform a basic simulation to quantify how often the true orientation knowledge is deemed to be inconsistent with the learned essential graph. And when it is consistent, we quantify how often the true restricted essential graph is recovered, and how many tail- and arrow-oriented edgemarks agree between the true and learned restricted essential graphs.

We generate a DAG with R’s
pcalg::randomDAG
with
n
nodes and probability,
p=0.2
. We choose
L=3
root or confounder nodes as latent nodes to generate the MAG
\mathcal{M}
, and the corresponding essential graph,
\mathcal{G}
. We pick
k\%=20\%
of non-identified edgemarks in the essential graph as our orientation knowledge,
\mathcal{K}
. We learn the
\mathcal{K}
-restricted essential graph,
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{K}}
using Algorithm
2
. We also generate
m=1000
data points using the DAG with
pcalg::rmvDAG
, remove the variables corresponding to the latent nodes. We estimate the essential graph using
pcalg::fci
,
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
. And if
\mathcal{K}
is consistent with
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
, we also estimate
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{K}}
with Algorithm
2
. We repeat this for
T=100
simulations. In scenarios where we can learn
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{K}}
, we also count how many arrowheads and tails agree with
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{K}}
. The results are summarized in Table

Appendix F
Completeness of Edge Mark Orientations in Ancestral Partial Mixed Graphs with no Minimal Collider Paths

Consider a partial mixed graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
obtained as output of Algorithm
2
. We examine edge orientations of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
, which is the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that corresponds to the circle component of the essential ancestral graph
\mathcal{G}
. We show that edge orientations within these types graphs are complete using an argument similar to
Meek [1995]
.

Since the skeleton of such a graph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
is chordal
[
Zhang, 2008b
]
, we can construct a join trees on its maximal cliques (see Chapter 3.2 of
Lauritzen, 1996
, and Theorem
78
below). Similar to
Meek [1995]
, we define a total ordering of maximal cliques in a join tree and show that this ordering induces a partial ordering of nodes in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
that is consistent with prior edge mark orientations, maintains the ancestral property and does not introduce any minimal collider paths. Then, we show how to select two MAGs represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{C}
as extensions of these orderings with the required orientations of an edge in question.

Our main result is presented in Theorem
29
in Section
F.2
. A map of how all results in this section are used to prove Theorem
29
is given in Figure
16
of Section
F.2
. Throughout this section we also include examples for intermediate results and algorithms, concluding with Example
12
, which demonstrates the constructive process for obtaining the MAGs described in Theorem
29
.

In Table
4
below, we make explicit the connections between our results and that of
Meek [1995]
. The second column provides locations or specific references to results in this manuscript that are somewhat analogous to those of
Meek [1995]
. In our proofs, we identify an important gap in Lemma 6 of
Meek [1995]
. Namely, Lemma 6 of
Meek [1995]
cannot hold as stated, which we illustrate in Example
4
and the text following it. Since Lemmas 7, 8, and Theorem 4 of
Meek [1995]
rely on Lemma 6, their proofs also do not go through. Our Lemmas
84
and
85
present weaker versions of Lemmas 6 and 7 of
Meek [1995]
and we devise a different strategy for using their results that allows us to prove Theorem
29
. Since our setting is more general, our proof of Theorem
29
will also serve as a proof of Theorem 4 of
Meek [1995]
.

Meek [1995]

F.1
Section Specific Preliminaries

Definition 69

(Partial Order)
.

Consider a set of elements
\mathbf{V}
. A relation
\leq
, between the elements of
\mathbf{V}
is called a partial order if and only if for every
A,B,C\in\mathbf{V}

(i)

reflexive:
A\leq A
,

(ii)

antisymmetric: if
A\leq B
and
B\leq A
, then
A=B
, and

(iii)

transitive: if
A\leq B
and
A\leq C
, then
A\leq C
.

Remark 70
.

If a pairwise relation
\pi
on a set of elements of
\mathbf{V}
is a partial ordering, then for elements
A,B\in\mathbf{V}
such that
\pi(A,B)
holds, we will also write
A\leq_{\pi}B
. Note also, that not every two elements of
\mathbf{V}
need to be comparable to have a partial ordering on
\mathbf{V}
. For distinct elements
A
and
B
in
\mathbf{V}
, if
A\not\leq B
and
B\not\leq A
, then we say that
A
and
B
are incomparable and we denote this by
A\not\lessgtr B
or, equivalently,
B\not\lessgtr A

[
Trotter, 1992
]
.

Definition 71

(Extending Orders)
.

A partial order
\pi_{1}
is an extension of a partial order
\pi_{2}
if and only if
A\leq_{\pi_{2}}B
implies
A\leq_{\pi_{1}}B
.

Definition 72

(Compatible Order)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partial mixed graph. A partial order
\pi
over
\mathbf{V}
is compatible with
\mathcal{G}
if and and only if for any pair of nodes
A
and
B
in
\mathcal{G}

•

if
A\to B
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
A\leq_{\pi}B
,

•

if
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\leftarrow$}\put(3.0,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
, then
A\not\leq_{\pi}B
.

Definition 73

(Induced Orientation)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partially directed mixed graph and let
\leq_{\alpha}
be a partial order on
\mathbf{V}
that is compatible with
\mathcal{G}
. Then
\leq_{\alpha}
induces a partial orientation as follows:

•

if
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(1.0,1.0){\circle{1.0}}\put(1.5,1.0){\line{1}{0}{2.4}}\put(2.9,0.0){$\bullet$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
and
A\leq_{\alpha}B
, or
\alpha(A,B)
, then orient
A\to B
.

The graph resulting from applying the above procedure is called
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
.

Lemma 74
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V,E})
be a partially directed ancestral mixed graph. Let
\pi
be a relation on the nodes of
\mathcal{G}
induced by the ancestral relationships. That is
\pi(A,B)
if and only if
A\in\An(B,\mathcal{G})
.
Then
\pi
is a partial ordering of
\mathbf{V}
that is compatible with
\mathcal{G}
.

By definition, every node in
\mathcal{G}
is an ancestor of itself, hence
\pi
is a reflexive relationship. To show that
\pi
is antisymmetric note that
\mathcal{G}
is ancestral, so if
A\in\An(B,\mathcal{G})
, that is
\pi(A,B)
and
B\in\An(A,\mathcal{G})
, that is
\pi(B,A)
holds, we must have
A\equiv B
.
The transitive property also holds by definition. Therefore,
\pi
is a partial ordering that is naturally compatible with
\mathcal{G}.

Definition 75

(Tree Graph)
.

A graph
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
is a tree if for any pair of nodes
A,B\in\mathbf{V}
, there is exactly one path
p=\langle A=V_{1},\dots,B=V_{k}\rangle
in
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition 76

(Join Tree Graph)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
be a graph. A join tree graph
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
for
\mathcal{G}
is an undirected tree graph whose nodes
\mathbf{C}
are a partition of
\mathbf{V}
with the following properties:

(i)

for set of nodes
\mathbf{A}\subseteq\mathbf{V}
that forms a maximal clique in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathbf{A}\equiv\mathcal{C}_{i}
, for some
\mathcal{C}_{i}\in\mathbf{C}
, and

(ii)

(running intersection) for each pair
\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\in\mathbf{C}
such that
A\in(\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\mathcal{C}_{j})\subseteq\mathbf{V}
,
each node
\mathcal{C}_{k}
on the unique path between
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{j}
in
\mathcal{T}
also contains
A
.

Remark 77
.

Join trees are sometimes also called junction trees or chordal trees, due to the fact that only chordal graphs have a join tree.
We state the original result of
Beeri et al. [1983]
in Lemma
78
. We refer the reader to
Jensen and Jensen [1994]
and
Lauritzen [1996]
for a modern treatment of join trees and how to construct them.

Lemma 78

(Theorem 3.4 of
Beeri et al. [1983]
)
.

A graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
has a join tree if and only if
\mathcal{G}
is chordal.

\Lambda_{ij}
notation.
Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
be a graph with a chordal skeleton. For maximal cliques
\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\subseteq\mathbf{V}
, we will use
\Lambda_{ij}
to denote their intersection, that is,
\Lambda_{ij}=\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\mathcal{C}_{j}
.

Definition 79

(
\gamma
-relation)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph such that the skeleton of
\mathcal{G}
is chordal and
\mathcal{G}
contains no minimal collider paths. Let
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
be an undirected join tree graph for
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\in\mathbf{C},
and
\Lambda_{ij}=\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\mathcal{C}_{j}
.
We define a relation
\gamma
on the nodes of
\mathcal{T}
as follows:
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
if and only if

(i)

\Lambda_{ij}\neq\varnothing
,

(ii)

for all
B\in\Lambda_{ij}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\Lambda_{ij}
,
B\to C
is in
\mathcal{G}
, and

(iii)

there exist nodes
A\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\setminus\Lambda_{ij}
and
B\in\Lambda_{ij}
such that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 80

(Partially Directed Join Tree)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph such that the skeleton of
\mathcal{G}
is chordal and
\mathcal{G}
contains no minimal collider paths. Let
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
be an undirected join tree graph for
\mathcal{G}
and let
\gamma
be a relation on the nodes of
\mathcal{T}
defined in Definition
79
.
We define a partially directed join tree graph
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{{}^{\prime\prime}})
as follows:

(i)

The skeleton of
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
is identical to the skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
.

(ii)

Edge
\langle\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j}\rangle
in
\mathcal{T}
corresponds to:

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
if
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
,

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{j}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
if
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{C}_{i})
, and

•

\mathcal{C}_{i}-\mathcal{C}_{j}
in
\mathcal{T}_{\gamma}
if neither
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
nor
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{j},\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

Remark 81
.

Note that the partially or fully directed trees we consider are not always arborescences in the graph theory sense. Meaning that our definition of a partially directed tree allows for more than one root node.

Definition 82

(Anchored Tree)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
be a partially directed tree graph and let
\mathcal{C}_{0}\in\mathbf{C}
. We say that
\mathcal{T}
is anchored around
\mathcal{C}_{0}
if
\PossAn(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{T})=\An(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{T})
.

Definition 83

(Join Tree Induced Edge Orientations)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{E})
be an ancestral partial mixed graph such that the skeleton of
\mathcal{G}
is chordal and
\mathcal{G}
contains no minimal collider paths. Let
\mathcal{T}=(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
be a partially directed join tree graph for
\mathcal{G}
(Definition
80
) and suppose that
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
is a partial ordering compatible with
\mathcal{T}
, such that
\mathcal{T}_{\pi_{\mathcal{T}}}
is a directed graph with no colliders. Then,
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
induces orientations on the nodes of
\mathcal{G}
using the following rule:

(i)

if
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
,
then for all
B\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
C\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
, orient
B\to C
.

The graph obtained as a result of this operation is called
\mathcal{G}_{\pi}
.

Proof structure of Theorem

Let
\mathcal{T}_{0}
be a partially directed join tree of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
(Definition
80
).
There is at least one clique
\mathcal{C}_{0}
such that
A,B\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
. We will first transform the partially directed join tree
\mathcal{T}_{0}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
into another partially directed join tree of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
called
\mathcal{T}_{1}
using the
transformTree
algorithm (Algorithm
6
in Section
F.4
), that is,
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\texttt{transformTree}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0})
. By Corollary
93
, the partially directed join tree
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is anchored around clique
\mathcal{C}_{0}
(Definition
82
), meaning that
\PossAn(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1})=\An(\mathcal{C}_{0},\mathcal{T}_{1})
. Furthermore, there are no unshielded colliders in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, or paths of the form
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}-\dots-\mathcal{C}_{k}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{l}
.
Note that unshielded colliders, or paths of the form
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{C}_{j}-\dots-\mathcal{C}_{k}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{l}
can occur in an arbitrarily chosen partially directed join tree as per Figures
19
-
21
(see also the associated examples for more details).
To construct join tree
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, Algorithm
6
relies on a few supporting algorithms (Algorithms
4
,
5
) and results in Sections
F.3
and
F.4
.

Next, we close the orientations in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
using algorithm
orientTree
(Algorithm
7
in Section
F.5
), to construct a directed join tree
\mathcal{T}
, that is,
\mathcal{T}=\texttt{orientTree}(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{0})
. Let
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
be a partial order compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.
By case
(ii)
of Lemma
95
,
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
induces edge orientations that are compatible with
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
through the process described in Definition
83
.

Therefore, let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
be the graph obtained from applying
\pi_{\mathcal{T}}
to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as in Definition
83
. Then
\langle A,B\rangle
is of the same form in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
(
(iii)
of Lemma
95
).
Furthermore, by case
(vii)
of Lemma
95
,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
is an ancestral partial mixed graph with no minimal collider paths and edge orientations closed under
R2
, and
R8
.
Additionally, any ancestral directed mixed graph
\mathcal{M}
that is represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
will be a MAG represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Observe that all edges in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
that are between two cliques are invariant. All variant edges (
[formula]
or
) are only present inside of cliques of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
. Therefore, to construct a MAG
\mathcal{M}
represented by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
with the desired orientation of
\langle A,B\rangle
edge, we now only need to orient
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
into an ancestral directed mixed graph. For this, it is enough to ensure that no directed or almost directed cycle is created within the maximal cliques of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
when orienting it into
\mathcal{M}
. To do this, we rely on Lemmas
96
and
97
in Section
F.6
, which give us two alternate procedures for orienting partially oriented cliques in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\pi}
with desired edge marks on
\langle A,B\rangle
.

General Partially Directed Join Tree Properties

Lemma 84
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an ancestral partial mixed graph with a chordal skeleton such that
\mathcal{G}
has no minimal collider paths such that the orientations in
\mathcal{G}
are closed under
R1
and
R11
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be a join tree for
\mathcal{G}
and
\gamma
a relation as defined in Definition
79
.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{j}
be adjacent in
\mathcal{T}
, and suppose that there is an
unshielded triple
\langle A,B,C\rangle
such that
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
in
\mathcal{G}
,
and
A,B\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
B,C\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
,
A\notin\mathcal{C}_{j}
,
C\notin\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Then
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
.

Used in proof of Lemma

.

Since
A\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.2,0.0){$\bullet$}\put(1.0,0.0){$\rightarrow$}\end{picture}B
is in
\mathcal{G}
and since
\Lambda_{ij}\neq\varnothing
, for
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
it is enough to show that for all
D\in\Lambda_{ij}
,
E\in\mathcal{C}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
D\to E
is in
\mathcal{G}
. There are three cases:

(i)

If
D\equiv B
, then for
E\equiv C
, or
E\notin\Adj(A,\mathcal{G})
,
\langle A,D,E\rangle
, forms an unshielded triple in
\mathcal{G}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
does not contain unshielded colliders, by
R1
, we conclude that

D\to E
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

(ii)

For
D\not\equiv B
but
E\equiv C
, we have that
\langle B,D\rangle
edge is in
\mathcal{G}
since
B,D\in\Lambda_{ij}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
does not contain unshielded colliders or longer minimal collider paths, we conclude by
R11
(see Figure
17(a)
) that
D\to C
, that is
D\to E
is in
\mathcal{G}
.

(iii)

For
D\not\equiv B
and
E\not\equiv C
, we know that,
\langle B,D\rangle
edge is in
\mathcal{G}
since
B,D\in\Lambda_{ij}
and also that
\langle D,C\rangle
,
\langle E,C\rangle
are in
\mathcal{G}
, since
B,C,D,E\in\mathcal{C}_{j}.

If
A\notin\Adj(E,\mathcal{G})
, then as in the cases above, by
R1

B\to E
is in
\mathcal{G}
and by
R11
,
D\to E
is also in
\mathcal{G}
and we are done.

Otherwise,
A\in\Adj(E,\mathcal{G})
as in Figure
17(b)
.
However, this case is not possible. For sake of contradiction assume that this is possible.
Note that
A,B,D,E
form a clique in
\mathcal{G}
, but since
E\notin\mathcal{C}_{i}
, there must be another maximal clique in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{C}_{k}
that is a node in
\mathcal{T}
, such that
A,B,D,E\in\mathcal{C}_{k}.

Furthermore,
C\notin\mathcal{C}_{k}
, because
A\notin\Adj(C,\mathcal{G})
.

There cannot be a path from
\mathcal{C}_{k}
to
\mathcal{C}_{j}
in
\mathcal{T}
that contains
\mathcal{C}_{i}
as that violates the running intersection property (
\mathcal{C}_{k}\cap\mathcal{C}_{j}\subseteq\{B,D,E\}\not\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{i}
as
E\notin\mathcal{C}_{i}
).

Similarly, there is no path from
\mathcal{C}_{i}
to
\mathcal{C}_{k}
in
\mathcal{T}
that contains
\mathcal{C}_{j}
as that also violates the running intersection property (
\mathcal{C}_{i}\cap\mathcal{C}_{k}\subseteq\{A,B,D\}\not\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{j}
since
A\notin\mathcal{C}_{j}
).

And since we assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{j}
are adjacent in
\mathcal{T}
there cannot be a path from
\mathcal{C}_{i}
to
\mathcal{C}_{j}
that contains
\mathcal{C}_{k}
. Thus, we have a contradiction to
A\in\Adj(E,\mathcal{G})
.

Therefore, we have shown that
\gamma(\mathcal{C}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{j})
.
